ALLWAYS / SOL — COMPLETE PUBLISHED CRITIC RECORDS

23 completed adaptations plus058 unfinished.78 record files follow verbatim. Some aggregate records repeat individual rounds. Bounded visual passes do not certify full audiovisual or original-prompt acceptance.


===== 055 / Turn / REVIEW.txt =====
ALLWAYS 055 / TURN / V03

A 55-second adaptation of SCION 055 (Words — Polysemic Relay).
One film, eight passages, seven source clips. The organizing word appears only after the image sequence. No synthetic imagery. Aspect ratio preserved; low-resolution archival clips upscaled without generative restoration.

V03 changes from recovered V02:
- Removes opening explanatory title so the viewer can infer the word.
- Holds the radio through its illuminated state, ending before the next source action.
- Uses 11–17 seconds of the dance excerpt to emphasize the visible turn.
- Adds a newly composed instrumental score. It is neither source sound nor reconstructed historical sound.
- Silences the page passage (35.6–40.6) and the final look (46.6–48.88).

SELF-REVIEW / INCONCLUSIVE
Inspected sampled frames from all seven recovered clips and eight-frame motion strips from reel, radio, page and dance. Reviewed sampled final-output frames; this is not a claim of continuous normal-speed viewing or an independent viewer test.
Strongest proposed pivot: 17.2–28.8, turning a control changes the radio's visible state.
Largest gap: reel and clay repeat rotation; their semantic separation is weaker than the radio's switch to 'turn on'.
Observable repair condition: a viewer should infer at least two meanings without the end title. If the viewer reports only circles/spinning, replace one rotational shot with a visibly established 'take turns' action.
The return uses an overlapping interval from the same child excerpt; no later historical event or changed emotional state is claimed.

Prompt departures remain explicit:
- TURN adapts the prompt's proposed DRAW; this does not satisfy the literal drawing/curtain/drawer proof.
- Source audio has been omitted; required source-sound bridges remain untested.
- Independent word-identification and emotional-direction judgments remain pending.
- Source rights are unverified; the ledger records access/provenance, not permission clearance.

Fresh forage during this pass:
Three Moving Image Archive searches: 'a hand spinning a ball', 'people taking turns playing a game', and 'a hand drawing a line on paper'. Each returned 24 candidates. Original responses are included. Thumbnail requests and the site's poster route failed with access errors, so these fresh candidates were NOT visually verified and were NOT inserted into the film. The film uses inspected recovered Cineosis cache excerpts only.

REBUILD
Unzip to an empty folder. With Python 3, numpy, scipy, Pillow, ffmpeg and ffprobe installed, run:
  python3 film55/make.py
The script expects the bundled editkit paths relative to its parent folder. It recreates the score, picture assembly, ledger and MP4. No online source downloads are required.


===== 055 / Turn / v04/ALLWAYS_055_CRITIC_RECORD.txt =====
ALLWAYS 055 / TURN / ACTUAL CRITIC RECORD

V03: FAIL. V04: PASS from fresh blind critic; QUALIFIED PASS from original critic.
Scope: visual word-relay. No independent audio audition or real-time audiovisual screening occurred.
Reports below are the reviewers' actual texts, retained with limitations and disagreements.

V04 final runtime: 47.04 seconds. Title-free image sequence: 41.64 seconds.
Technical checks are recorded separately and are not artistic approval.

Source correction: V03's radio/turn-on reading was unsupported. The critic observed a lens/cap demonstration instead; this source and the unclear cup passage were removed. V04 makes no radio/switch claim.


===== critic_blind/report.txt =====
BLIND VISUAL CRITIC — first inspection, without concept explanation
Verdict: FAIL for a fully legible visual word-relay; recognizable organizing idea, but material gaps.

Method and limits: Inspected the full 48.96-second temporal span as ordered 4 fps contact sheets (six sheets, 196 sampled frames), plus larger 2 fps frames of the camera section. Scene-change decoding supplied precise boundary times. I did not watch native real-time playback or listen to the audio. I can assess shot order, visible action and approximate durations; I cannot certify sound, real-time rhythm, or audiovisual synchronization. The existence of an audio stream is not evidence of its perceptual effectiveness.

Unprompted organizing word: TURN is my best inference. A child turns toward us; a reel rotates; a cup/hand is manipulated; camera lens is covered; a car travels along a bend; a page turns; partners turn in dance; the child returns. “Turn” connects (1) physical rotation, (2) change of direction/orientation, and (3) an idiomatic action, turning a page. These are distinguishable verbal uses, though several shots repeat literal rotation rather than making a strong semantic leap. I did not independently discover a clear extra sense such as taking one's turn or a turn of fortune.

Shot/action legibility:
0.00–4.20: Child raises a hand to a door then turns head/body toward camera. The turn is visible and the image is emotionally available, though knocking/touching a door competes as the primary action.
4.20–10.20: Person handles a film reel while pulling loose film. Reel movement is visible, but unspooling/pulling film is at least as salient as a deliberate turn. A usable rotation link.
10.20–17.20: Woman at cup and saucer reaches and manipulates the cup area. This is visually soft and obscure. I can identify drinking/handling/stirring more readily than a specific new meaning of turn. Too much duration for so small an action.
17.20–28.80: Box camera and hand holding a round cap. Roughly the first eight seconds show almost no perceptible change. Around 25.5–27.0 the hand moves over the lens; it is capped by about 27.0. Covering/closing the lens is legible. A twisting or turning action is not legible from these frames. This is the largest break in the relay.
28.80–35.60: Inside a moving car. Road and scenery shift; steering/rounding a bend is plausible. The dark driver/wheel and frontal framing weaken how decisively one reads “turn” instead of simply “drive.” Small animated figures add competing activity.
35.60–40.60: Open book; hand crosses and turns a page around 38–39 seconds. The clearest conventional verbal action, and the most distinct meaning from rotation.
40.60–46.60: Dance partners take hold and rotate. Action is legible; the later rotation is stronger than the opening approach. This reads as another literal bodily turn.
46.60–48.88: Child repeated from the opening, now already facing toward us for much of the return. The loop is evident.

Strongest cut: 35.60, car to book. Both change the direction in which one proceeds, and the move from movement through space to a page offers a distinct verbal use. The new page action takes several seconds to arrive, so this could be much sharper if the page begins to lift just after the cut. Runner-up: 4.20, child's bodily turn into rotating reel, an immediately interpretable transfer from person to mechanism.
Weakest cut: 17.20, cup to camera. A dim hand/cup action gives way to a largely still demonstration of a camera cap; neither side delivers an unmistakable turning motion. The camera's 11.60-second allocation dominates without adding a clear new meaning. 28.80 inherits the same problem: a lens being covered does not visibly hand off a turn to the car.

Does the last return change the first image? Only weakly. Following dance, it suggests a child's gesture may be another bodily turn and gives a formal circle. It does not make the child's initial door gesture mean something new; it chiefly repeats it. The final child is no longer doing the clearest initial turning movement. The lens/camera material might invite a glance toward the viewer, but that inference is mine rather than a clearly built reversal. The current ending establishes recurrence, not a strong retroactive transformation.

Actionable repair: First remove or radically shorten the long camera hold. If TURN is intended, select an unmistakable twist of a focusing ring/crank or another visible use of the word; a cap being placed is insufficient. Trim cup to a demonstrable rotation or replace it with a different sense (taking turns in play, a visible change in fortune, turning something inside out). Enter the car at the strongest curve/steering action and the book just before the page lifts. For the return, place the exact earlier bodily turn after the dance's rotation, or use a later child gesture that makes the door a threshold/refusal/choice established elsewhere. A stronger final return needs accumulated meaning, not just the original image repeated.

This is not an all-purpose failure of the film: the organizing relation is discoverable and the book/dance/child chain has promise. It does not yet pass the demanding criteria of every action being legible, a relay spanning distinct meanings, and a final return that changes the first image. Sound could help the viewing experience, but cannot be credited in this visual-only judgment.


===== critic_blind/additional_report.txt =====
ADDITIONAL SOURCE REVIEW — independent visual inspection
Method: Entire 255.mp4 examined as ordered 8 fps frames; entire 09.mp4 as ordered 10 fps frames, with every native frame at 1.50–2.10 enlarged and time-labeled. Entire 15.mp4 examined at 4 fps. No native playback or audio listening.

BALL DRILL, 255.mp4
Qualified PASS as a source for taking turns. This is materially stronger than the cup or camera. A front child performs the throw/receive action, moves aside, and another child advances from the small line into the same active position. That pattern repeats. Waiting peers remain visible. The image can convey an allocated opportunity to act, a distinct meaning of turn rather than simple rotation.
Retain two cycles: approximately 0.35–5.70 seconds gives the first exchange, departure, successor, and second departure. Exact entry can move earlier to preserve anticipation. Under ~3 seconds would risk reading only as a ball pass or ordinary movement. The final ~7-second region begins changing composition and is unnecessary for this meaning. Do not claim all the children follow a perfectly explicit formal queue; their overlaps and circulation remain somewhat busy.

CHILD, 09.mp4
A usable nonrepeating split lies at 1.768 seconds, immediately before the visible return of the head toward the camera. Native rate is 29.97 fps. Last safely unturned native frame: 1.735 seconds (frame 52, if zero-based). Next frame 1.768 (53) begins the change; 1.802–2.069 makes it clear. Opening could use frames 14–52 (~0.467–1.768 exclusive); ending resumes at frame 53 with no overlap. Keep ending to ~3.7–4.2 seconds for a visible resolved look and hand lowering, depending on desired duration. The first ~0.0–0.4 seconds already contains a turn toward the door; omit it if the goal is to withhold the physical turn itself. Use native-frame selection before any frame-rate conversion if exact non-overlap matters.

Will this materially transform the return?
It certainly improves the construction: an unfinished gesture becomes completed, instead of repeating a turn already shown. The child's initially unreadable raised hand/door action acquires a possible relation to the waiting children: waiting one's turn, wanting access, then turning toward us. That is a plausible emotional inflection. But the split alone guarantees formal completion, not semantic transformation. The child is alone; no queue, partner, shared object, or refused entry establishes that the child is waiting for an allocated turn. The ball drill does not prove that meaning in another shot. I would not call this a guaranteed pass of a strict changed-return criterion before seeing the new assembly.
Best repair within these sources: put ball drill directly before the final child, letting a child's completed action/exit meet the solitary child's raised hand, then the turn toward the viewer. Preserve enough of the ball scene to establish succession first. Opening should emphasize the unanswered door/raised hand rather than showing a turn. This yields a tentative contrast between group participation and solitary waiting. It is a defensible reading, but don't describe it as objectively established exclusion or refusal. If a robust semantic transformation is essential, obtain an image with an actual wait/access/exchange relation, not merely a delayed head movement.

PHONOGRAPH CANDIDATE, 15.mp4
0–~6.3: Small open-lidded apparatus on table; largely still, framed over a shoulder.
~6.3–9.6: Cut to portable record apparatus. Hand enters at right, grips and lifts a black corded receiver/pickup-shaped component, carries it forward, rotates its round end toward camera, then takes it out of frame. This is plainly handling and removing/lifting a component. No switch/dial turn or unambiguous on/off operation is established.
~9.8–13.2: Case and disc remain in view, little action.
~13.2–15.0: Front grille close-up.
~15.0–17.0: Close view of disc with changing highlights/surface patterns, legible rotation.
~17.0–20.18: Wider low angle of rotating disc.
Verdict: Usable literal rotation, not an established new sense of turn. I reject a claim that the hand visibly turns the machine on/off. A transition from handling a component to a spinning disc might imply starting playback, but that is an inference and not the particular action shown. It can replace an illegible rotation with a legible one, but would duplicate reel's mechanical rotation and consume time better devoted to the clear taking-turns sense. Sound/music linkage could give it another purpose, which I have not judged.


===== critic_blind/v04_report.txt =====
V04 VISUAL CRITIC REPORT
Verdict: QUALIFIED PASS for the visual word-relay. The major visual failures identified in the first version are repaired. The ending now has a defensible changed relation to the opening, though it remains the most interpretation-dependent part.

Method: Inspected all seven ordered 5 fps contact sheets spanning the complete 41.72-second film. Scene-change decoding provided boundary times. I did not watch native real-time playback or hear sound. No pass is claimed for audio, audiovisual synchronization, or fine real-time rhythm. This is a return review by the earlier critic, not a newly blind inference.

Actual sequence and legibility:
0.00–1.32: Child faces closed door and raises/reaches with a hand. The turn toward us is withheld. Reads as seeking attention/access; “waiting” remains an inference.
1.32–2.92: Wider view of boy handling reel; 2.92–9.32: close reel rotation and film unspooling. Mechanical rotation is unmistakable across the section, although pulling/unwinding film remains its primary practical action. This is considerably longer than needed to identify it.
9.32–16.72: Car travels along road, with the steering wheel visibly changing position toward the end. This is stronger than in the first assembly because the actual steering action is included. Rotation of wheel plus change of travel direction is legible; the little animated figure remains a distraction, but not fatal.
16.72–22.12: Book, hand lifts and turns a leaf at approximately 19.0–19.7; 22.12–22.72: brief dark cover/edge view. Turning a page is completely legible. The final dark insert does not add a new meaning.
22.72–31.12: Dance partners approach, take hold and rotate. Bodily turning is clear by the later half. The approach also prepares the social meaning of participation, but it is relatively long.
31.12–38.32: Ball drill. Multiple children advance into and leave the active position as a ball is exchanged. The complete repeated succession is retained. This establishes “taking turns,” a genuinely distinct meaning from physical rotation, much more effectively than the deleted camera/cup material.
38.32–41.64: Child resumes the unfinished movement and turns toward the viewer, then lowers the hand while holding our gaze. The action is completed rather than replayed. 41.64–41.72 black.

Organizing word and distinct meanings: TURN. Mechanical/bodily rotation; turning a vehicle/change of direction; turning a page; taking one's turn. This version no longer depends on guessing that a lens cap being placed is a twist or that an obscure cup manipulation is a turn.

Strongest cut: 38.32, ball drill to child. The ball drill first makes a turn into a social opportunity to participate; the child at the closed door then turns toward us. The opening gesture can now be revisited as an unanswered request or wait, rather than just another specimen of rotation. That interpretation is modest and poetic, not a claim that we have seen literal exclusion. The direct adjacency matters: an intervening rotation shot would weaken it. Second strongest: 16.72, steering/travel into page, moving from directional change through space to an ordered next unit.
Weakest cut: 22.72, dark book edge into the dance's preliminary approach. It momentarily loses the specific action being relayed. The meaning recovers when the couple turns, but a tighter entry on taking hold/first rotation would reduce the wait. The child-to-reel cut at 1.32 is deliberately less semantically immediate because the first bodily turn is withheld; the ending repairs that incompletion.

Does the ending change the beginning? Qualified yes. The opening is now an unresolved outward reach at a door; after scenes of ordered passage and shared participation, the child turns away from the door and meets the spectator. The shift from the group's sequential turns to one child's turn toward us creates a relation the opening alone did not have. It is more than a visual loop because the movement was withheld and the social sense immediately precedes it. I would not claim a strong narrative transformation, a proved refusal of entry, or “the child finally gets a turn”: no one opens the door or exchanges something with the child. Those stronger readings remain INCONCLUSIVE. The film supports asking for attention and redirecting attention; its restraint is acceptable for a visual poem.

Remaining repair if revising further: shorten reel by roughly 2–3 seconds, keeping two clear rotations and a readable film pull; trim dance entry by roughly 1.5–2 seconds while retaining taking hold and rotation; consider removing the 22.12–22.72 dark book insert. Do not shorten ball drill so much that succession ceases to be apparent. These are economy improvements, not remaining mandatory fixes for basic visual-relay legibility.

Summary of original fail conditions: camera ambiguity removed; cup ambiguity removed; distinct social sense added and shown through repeated action; car's steering now visible; final child movement no longer duplicated; final return gains a plausible social/attentional inflection. On those visual grounds this revised sequence passes, with a modest rather than conclusive transformed-return claim. Audio remains unassessed.


===== critic_round2/report.txt =====
BLIND FILM CRITIC REPORT
Verdict: PASS for the visual word-relay, with pacing reservations.

Source viewed: the supplied title-free film_v04.mp4, duration 41.72 seconds. No project files, prior explanations, or intended concept were read. Viewing method: 167 extracted frames at four frames per second, inspected in temporal order across seven contact sheets, plus enlarged individual frames. This is dense frame inspection, not native video playback. The audio stream was identified by metadata but was NOT heard. Judgments about continuous motion, rhythm, and sound therefore have that limitation. Timecodes below are sampled observations, accurate to the quarter-second viewing grid rather than claims of frame-exact edit positions.

INDEPENDENT VERBAL INFERENCE
The organizing word is TURN. It is discoverable without a title. The clearest literal phrase is 'turn a page' (around 19.00-19.50); the reel and steering give mechanical turns, the dancers give bodily turns, and the school game adds the distinct social meaning 'take turns.' These are more than several similarly circular objects.

PASSAGE AUDIT
0.00-1.00: A small child faces a closed door and raises a hand. This does not itself plainly say 'turn'; I initially read reaching/knocking/wanting entry. It functions as a withheld action rather than a self-sufficient word example. Its support depends on the ending.
1.25-9.00: The boy handles and winds film on a reel, with sustained close views of hands and reel. This establishes turning as material manipulation. Legibility is strong; length is excessive for the semantic information supplied.
9.25-16.50: The view from inside a moving car develops into a visible change of course, especially the fence moving across the windscreen and the driver's wheel movement around 15.00-16.50. This makes 'turn' a direction change. The miniature animated figure intermittently overlaid near the left/bottom is distracting: its link to the actual steering action is less immediate than the photographic action.
16.75-22.50: A reader's hands and open book culminate in a clear page turn around 19.00-19.50, followed by the book closing near 22.00. This is the strongest lexical confirmation. The page turn translates rotation into progress through a sequence, not simply another wheel.
22.75-30.75: Partners come together and rotate while dancing. The film moves from turning an object to turning with another person. Approach/setup around 23.00-26.00 delays the decisive action but does make partnership legible.
31.00-38.00: Boys pass a ball and shift their positions in a gym. This supports both bodily turning and 'taking turns': one participant yields his position and another receives the action. This is the most valuable semantic expansion beyond physical rotation, although an even cleaner complete handoff would sharpen it.
38.25-41.50: Return to the child at the door, now turning to face the viewer. The child is no longer just a repeated opening composition. The withheld turn becomes visible, and looking outward supplies a human endpoint after the mechanical and social turns.

TRANSITIONS
The joins do more than repeat shapes. Reel to car keeps a rotating mechanism but changes its consequence from gathering film to changing a route. Car to book changes direction into progression through pages. Book to dance changes from manipulating an object to coordinating bodies. Dance to school game expands into allocated participation. These associations are intelligible through their sequence even though most joins are plain cuts and several actions take too long to arrive. The word relation carries the montage more convincingly than any tight movement match.

FINAL RETURN
The ending changes the opening's significance enough to pass. At first the child is defined by an outward reach toward a closed surface. At the end the child turns away from that surface and meets our gaze; following the intervening examples, the human act reads as the final sense of TURN. This is a modest reframe rather than a dramatic revelation. It should not be described as proof that the door opened or that anyone answered: neither event is visible.

STRENGTHS
- 'Turn a page' anchors the otherwise abstract montage in a familiar verbal use.
- The gym passage supplies a different meaning, taking turns, and keeps the film from being only a catalogue of rotation.
- The closing child actually performs a new action, so the return earns its place.

BIGGEST GAP AND CONCRETE REPAIR
The reel occupies roughly eight seconds and the car another seven; both communicate their sense before their sections finish. The opening is consequently held in suspension through a long mechanical first third. Tighten the reel to one clear winding cycle plus the handoff to the car, and begin the car later so its visible steering/corner action arrives promptly. Preserve a complete, unmistakable ball handoff in the gym and the child's final turn. These cuts would make each new meaning feel like a relay rather than an excerpt with waiting time. If only one repair is possible, shorten the reel section by approximately three seconds.

Overall: the title-free visual sequence passes the organizing-word test on its own evidence. This verdict does not certify sound design or native-playback pacing, neither of which was auditioned here.


===== 056 / In Different Hands / ALLWAYS_056_CRITIC_RECORD.txt =====
ALLWAYS 056 / IN DIFFERENT HANDS / ACTUAL CRITIC RECORD

Existing UNEQUAL WORK draft: FAIL. Rebuilt V02: PASS from both visual critics.
Scope: visual pairing, not a full audiovisual screening. Neither critic listened to audio or watched native real-time playback. One compared the entire sequential control. Their actual observations and disagreements remain below.

35-second finished short: 30 seconds paired images and 5 seconds closing title. This archive-led adaptation does not claim the literal chair/knot actions or the prescribed 50-second comparison. It uses six independently identified clip records. No scene is presented as a shared encounter across recordings.

Source corrections: no radio/turn-on claim; the white shape in the projector excerpt is a human glove, not autonomous machine output. Mirror subject touches collar/chin; not hair brushing. Final freeze is editorial and labelled HELD IMAGES. Exact identity of upper final apparatus is uncertain from its close view; report readers should retain that uncertainty.


===== critic056/baseline_report.txt =====
INDEPENDENT BASELINE VISUAL CRITIC
Film: ALLWAYS_056_UNEQUAL_WORK.mp4, 36.000 s, 960x540, 25 fps.
VERDICT: FAIL for visual pairing. This verdict addresses visuals only.

METHOD
Inspected this film before consulting any project explanation, ledger, source title, or prior review. No native real-time playback or audio listening. Inspected all 144 ordered samples at 4 fps (00:00.000 through 00:35.750), plus 1 fps overview sheets, full-resolution individual frames at 00:02.400 and 00:12.000, and a 390-pixel-wide mobile-sized frame at 00:22.000. This permits visual-order/action assessment but not exact velocity or audio assessment. Cut times reported to the nearest inspected quarter second; observed sequences begin at 00:02.000, 00:10.000, 00:20.000, and 00:32.000.

OBSERVED SEQUENCES AND JUDGMENTS
00:00.000–00:01.960: UNEQUAL WORK opening card.
00:02.000–00:09.960: On the left, a seated woman brings a small implement toward a small light cup-like object above a dark circular work surface; she lowers her face toward the work and reaches outward again. On the right, rows of red hanging, tapered forms rise and fall beneath brass-colored collars; the camera progressively isolates one. I can read a relation of fine intervention around a small circular object versus repeated mechanized intervention in rows. This does not mean the woman and machine are equivalent or share an event. Individual attention versus serial repetition is a potentially meaningful mismatch. However, at mobile scale the woman's implement and its effect are hard to resolve, so the first correspondence is tentative, not decisive. Person and workbench remain visible initially; right-side process context becomes thin in the final close view.
00:10.000–00:19.960: Left: a very large hand/finger rests on or guides the edge of a rectangular dark strip or plate; frame changes are slight. Right: elevated long view of people among repeated industrial workstations, with bodies leaning and reaching between machine structures. The right clearly retains an industrial workplace; the left removes the operator, surroundings, and function. No specific initial shared action is legible before the mismatch of scale appears. At mobile size the crowded right side becomes texture while the left reads as a static fingertip. This is the weakest pairing: a difference is immediately present but has not been earned through correspondence.
00:20.000–00:27.960: Left: hand near the top of a wood-grained box with a central dark circular fitting. Right: a reel-equipped green/yellow mechanism, with a white hand-shaped element moving at its front. The implied handmade/automatic movement relation is accessible in silhouette; the left initially has very little visible motion.
00:28.000–00:31.960: Left hand moves onto the dark fitting; by 00:30.000 a bright circular face remains after the hand withdraws. Right mechanism continues with the white shape held at its front while framing shifts to show more of the exposed works. This is the most promising divergence: a human hand completes a local change and exits, while the other mechanism continues. Yet the consequence and purposes of the two devices remain ambiguous. No wider operator context is supplied on the left.
00:32.000–00:35.960: Closing card says Comparison remains open and Different recordings, materials, purposes and tempos. This is a verbal qualification, not a visual final hold. It does not change an expectation set up by the opening: it explains how to regard the montage after the moving comparison has disappeared.

REQUESTED TESTS
First relation readable at intended mobile display size: FAIL / borderline first pair. Small implements and work effects are below useful mobile detail, although person versus serial machine is visible.
Mismatch meaningful rather than accidental: FAIL overall. First and last pairs have a possible meaningful tension; the middle pair supplies no clear shared action before asking for comparison.
Subjects/context retained: FAIL overall. First left and middle right retain setting; middle left and final left retain too little functional or human context to identify what the actions do.
Final hold changes expectations versus opening: FAIL. Closing text replaces rather than sustains the consequential visual divergence.

LARGEST CAUSAL DEFECT
The edit uses general contrasts of scale, person/machine, and image density before showing a common readable action. Consequently the middle ten seconds feel like juxtaposition selected by category, not a correspondence that breaks in a consequential way. Large unused black margins further suppress the fine evidence on a mobile display.

ACTIONABLE REPAIRS
1. Rebuild 00:10–00:20 around an observable common event: hands bring material into a fixture, press/turn, release, or inspect. Establish it on both sides before preserving a different outcome or duration. If the left strip cannot show a legible action and wider context, replace that excerpt.
2. Enlarge both source windows while retaining uncropped operator and surrounding machine/workbench detail. The current windows use roughly 150 px each in a 390 px mobile view, leaving critical action too small. Select sources whose decisive action remains legible at that scale.
3. Start the final pair early enough to identify the box's purpose through an actual wider view or prior action, and establish a clear repeated movement shared with the reel mechanism. Then let the hand stop/leave while the other continues. Preserve that asymmetric moving hold for several seconds before any credit/card.
4. End on the sustained image divergence rather than the explanatory card, or greatly shorten the card after the image has already changed the viewer's expectation.


===== critic056/source_report.txt =====
INDEPENDENT CANDIDATE SOURCE VISUAL REPORT
Method: looked at actual decoded images, not descriptions or titles. 1 fps ordered overview samples for each entire source; 4 fps ordered samples across 177, 310, 336, 176, and 41; full frame at 177 03.750. No audio listening or real-time playback. Timing precision is approximately 0.25 seconds; suggested boundaries should be conformed to exact source frames during editing. These are source-support judgments, not a pass of any unrendered film.

A. 336 + 45: SUPPORTS CORRESPONDENCE THEN DIVERGENCE
336 visible action: a woman at a stove lifts browned pieces from a large shallow pan with a broad slotted implement and places them on an already filled plate in front. Initial close view opens to include her body, stove, wall radiator, and room. She briefly reaches to a front stove control near 12–13 s. Further utensil-to-plate movement occurs around 14–17.5 s. At 18.25–19.25 s she grasps and lifts the plate, turns, and carries it out to the right. By approximately 21.75 s she has left the view; the pan and empty plate position remain through 22.75 s.
45 visible action: initially rectangular receiving trays pass under a dark overhead apparatus (00–04.5). From approximately 05.00 to 09.00 a row of brass-colored fittings above a moving pale receiving surface repeatedly extends reddish material down in tapered forms, then separates from it. After 09.00 framing tightens toward one fitting, while material extrusion/deposition continues until the source ends at 14.18. The broad row view makes repetition and receiving surface more legible than the final close view.
Useful paired interval: 336 14.00–22.75 against 45 05.00–13.75. First show at least one utensil-to-plate movement against material going to the receiving surface. Then preserve the divergence when the woman removes the plate and leaves while the row continues making deposits. This is stronger than merely calling both cooking: finite material transfer to one removable plate versus ongoing serial transfer to a moving surface is directly visible. Different materials, people, location, and events remain distinct. Do not identify the extruded substance from metadata, and do not claim the machine has no people elsewhere. Potential mobile risk: the implement and plate need enough pixels; keep woman's room context when she exits. Do not crop to her hand.

B. 177 + 310: SUPPORTS A BROAD SELF/OTHER RELATION; DOES NOT SUPPORT A STRONG COMPLETION CLAIM
177: one woman seen from behind and in a mirror. 00–02 s she turns toward the mirror. From about 02.50 s she runs her own hand across her upper chest/shoulder, then moves fingertips toward neck/chin at 04.75–05.75, then returns the hand down toward her collar/upper chest through 06.75. I do not see her combing hair, cutting hair, or using a brush. There is no clear finished grooming result or departure before the source cuts at 07.007.
310: 00.75–06.75 a man stands beside a seated child with a white cape, holds the top of the child's head/hair with one hand/comb, and brings a dark corded tool around the side/back of the child's hair with the other. Around 07 s the angle shifts closer to the child's front. The man continues working above/alongside the head to ~10.75. An optical dissolve around 11–12 s changes to a room with several people working on other seated people's heads through the end at 15.95.
Useful interval: 177 00.50–07.00 paired with 310 00.75–07.25 (or 310 04.00–10.50 if closer action is wanted). Verdict: PASS for visual pairing, with a weaker final pair.

I inspected the actual MP4 through an ordered 1 fps sheet, then 120 ordered samples at 4 fps covering 0–30 seconds, an additional dense ending sheet, and direct 720px/360px stills. That changes my opening expectation of an uninterrupted sequence of productive actions. Its force is modest rather than conclusive: the ending's strongest idea comes from the overt editorial hold plus the projector, rather than from an unmistakable action mismatch within the source footage.

HELD IMAGES is legible at the ending. The body/face relation survives reduced size better than the finer projector demonstration.

SCION058 Laughs — Audible Contagion
Independent context-forage critic

Scope and method
I inspected the complete cached source clips 229, 259, 39 and 403 using ordered contact sheets sampled every half second, from beginning to end. I inspected no builder plans or other critic reports. This is a visual/context review of source material, not a finished-film pass. This tool surface does not let me audition the audio. The files contain audio streams, but stream presence does not establish their contents. All laughter/audio claims below remain unverified.

229.mp4 — 13.614 seconds
A close view opens on a seated man with a moustache. His mouth changes shape repeatedly; his eyes look down and later up/right, and his head tilts toward the person beside/behind him near the end. The camera widens to reveal the adjacent man in a striped shirt, resting a hand against his chin/mouth. That second man remains comparatively still, blinking and changing his gaze. There is no visually clear spread of laughter between these people. The foreground mouth motion is compatible with speaking; I cannot distinguish laughter from speech using these frames. Neither a broad smile nor a shared laugh is firmly established. The hand at the other man's mouth is not proof of suppressed laughter.
No concrete mishap, injury, or failed action is visible in this short extract. However, the sustained speaking posture and downcast glances could belong to a serious exchange. Adding unrelated laughter could make the speaker appear to be ridiculed or make the other man appear to mock him. The actual topic and emotional stakes are unknown. Retain this pair's original situation; do not present an outsider in another clip as a listener or respondent.

259.mp4 — 8.942 seconds
A man enters through a door carrying a small dark case/device, looks into the room, closes or draws the door behind him, and walks across the room. A seated person near the staircase briefly turns or looks toward him. He smiles broadly as he approaches a seat, bends over the carried object, sits, and near the end raises a handset-like piece. The visible smile is sustained around the middle/end; a laugh is not distinguishable from smiling/talking. His bending is associated with handling the object and sitting; it is not visual proof of laughter pulses. The seated person's look is a within-scene reaction to the entrant, but its meaning cannot be called amusement from these frames.
No clear stumble, failure or embarrassment is visible. A wider narrative might explain the smile or object, but is absent here. Unrelated laughter under the object handling could manufacture a comic failure or imply the person near the staircase is laughing at him. Preserve the entrance/object-handling context if used.

39.mp4 — 8.141 seconds
A man sits behind a desk/table in a room with several people seated or moving in the foreground. He manipulates his hands or an object near his chest/table, then makes larger outward arm gestures. Several people turn or orient toward the desk; some stand or cross in front. A woman in a dark dress with a pale collar moves across the room near the end. The wide shot and limited resolution make facial expression difficult to judge. I cannot confidently identify a laugh, smiling contagion or a laugh response from anyone. The visible group orientation supports only that some people attend to the desk activity.
The desk activity may be a demonstration or presentation, but its exact purpose and whether it succeeds are unknown. This creates a particular mockery risk: imposed laughter could frame the seated demonstrator as incompetent, embarrassed or the butt of a joke without evidence. I see no decisive failure, humiliation, injury or distress, and cannot rule out narrative stakes outside this extract. Do not convert the group's looks into asserted laughter or connect them causally to unrelated clips.

403.mp4 — 6.206 seconds
A man in a dark vehicle-like interior repeatedly throws or tips his head back and forward. His mouth opens very widely; his eyes narrow or close and his face creases. The final frames show a sustained open-mouthed expression and conspicuous visible teeth. This is the strongest visual candidate for apparent laughter among the four extracts. Still, these silent sampled frames cannot certify whether it is laughter, shouting, a grimace, speech, or another vocal event. No other person is visibly established as reacting to him, so this clip supplies no visual evidence of contagion.
No external mishap or difficulty is shown. His exposed expression and teeth could become an object of ridicule if isolated, juxtaposed with unrelated onlookers, or scored with other people's laughter. The source does not establish that others mock him. Retain his own source sound/situation if an audio audition confirms the intended event.

Acoustic and duration verdict
NOT ESTABLISHED. I cannot certify any acoustic onset, rhythmic laugh pulse, inhalation, breath, recovery, shared timing, speaker identity, speech-to-laugh transition, or contagious audio handoff. A face, head movement, waveform/energy envelope, title or metadata is not evidence of any of those. I have not listened and cannot imply that I did.
The four complete source durations sum to approximately 36.90 seconds. They do not themselves supply 45 seconds of unique source time, even before any unusable material is removed. Repetition, held images, silence, titles or padding cannot establish a 45-second audible contagion chain. More source material plus actual auditory review is required for that claim. Even after audio audition, cross-source sequencing must not imply that unrelated people are responding to one another.

Review disposition

ALLWAYS 059 — CRITIC A, V01
Comparison verdict: REGISTERED VERSION STRONGER for the intended graphic-circle continuity. Visual-evidence verdict: qualified pass for central round-form recurrence and both context releases, with a substantial weak link at the bread. Overall experiential acceptance: INCONCLUSIVE, because I have not viewed either film in native normal-speed playback or heard its sound. These are separate judgments; the limitation does not establish either perceptual success or failure.

Independence/method: Read no build script or ledger. First impression came from all four registered ordered contact sheets, then all four control sheets. I subsequently extracted 24 larger time-selected frames from the actual registered MP4 and inspected an additional ordered 10 fps sequence at 32.0–34.4 seconds. Sheet times span both complete 38-second films. I received a stated intention/timing description before first inspection, so this is independent critical judgment, not a claim of fully blind concept discovery. No audio listening or native real-time video viewing occurred.

First visual impression: A central round object persists through different materials and functions: clock, black aperture, projector reel, grille, lamp, dark circular/elliptical object among transparent vessels. The bread/hands image breaks that recognition. The dinner view that follows reveals a practical setting and an action instead of a diagram. Later, round mechanisms, iris, record, reel and ball return to the graphic exercise; the ball is released into play. I see a deliberate pattern, not just an arbitrary miscellaneous montage.

Comparison with the control:
0.0–4.0: Registered clock→black aperture is conspicuously steadier in location and apparent diameter. Control aperture expands greatly relative to clock. Registered is stronger for visual carryover.
4.0–8.0: Registration emphasizes projector's top reel and the grille center; control gives more useful machine identity but distributes attention across multiple components. Registered is stronger for the specified graphic focus.
8.0–12.0: Registered lamp and dark object among vessels occupy a more stable target area. Their contours are less similar than the first pair: lamp is a bulbous rounded rectangle and dark object a horizontal ellipse. Control's paired lamps and repeated vessel pattern have compositional appeal of their own, but weaker single-anchor persistence.
12.0–15.0: Registered magnifies the bread/hand area. Control makes the food/table relation readable immediately. Neither offers a plainly unobstructed round bread outline. Registration supplies apparent target location without necessarily supplying perceived target identity.
20.0–24.8: Registered rotary-looking part→iris is a convincing change in material and function with the target sustained. Control shows an array of different parts before the eye, making the viewer choose which circle to carry across. Registered materially improves that relation.
24.8–29.6: Record label and handled reel are similarly centered; record label is a flattened ellipse and reel tilted. Useful perceptual family, not an optically identical circle.
29.6–33.2: Registered isolates the ball considerably better than control before gradually revealing people/court. Control begins with the action setting and therefore loses the reveal's force. Registered stronger.

Precise sequence:
0–2 clock; 2–4 dark aperture; 4–6 projector reel; 6–8 grille; 8–10 lamp; 10–12 dark round/oval form among vessels; 12–14 bread/hands freeze; 14–15 continuous widening release; 15–20 original meal action/context.
20–22.4 rotary-looking part; 22.4–24.8 iris; 24.8–27.2 record; 27.2–29.6 handled reel; 29.6–32 ball freeze; 32–33.2 widening release; 33.2–34.3 original ball action; 34.3–38 closing card.

Strongest relations: 2.0 clock→aperture clearly trades measured/informational surface for depth/darkness while keeping round attention. 22.4 mechanical rotary array→living iris is the strongest material/functional change. The 32–33.2 ball release makes the ball's placement in shared activity legible; the original motion resumes at 33.2 and the ball is no longer visible by roughly 33.4–33.5 in my sampled frames. This establishes disappearance, not a certified literal edge exit: a catch or occlusion is possible and requires native-frame trajectory checking. A person turns and begins leaving their position afterwards. I do not certify the moving-exit criterion from these samples.

Largest gap: 12–14 bread still, specifically the relation from 10–12. Hands cover and fragment the putative circular object, and pale food blends with fingers. I recognize bread handling/tearing, but cannot recover a confident single bread circumference or a persistent target comparable to the preceding black ellipse. The pattern has already released into an action image before the intended 14–15 context release. This weakens the contrast between geometric control and meaningful release. It is an observable perceptual issue, not resolved by numeric center/radius metadata.

What the bread release does well: 14–15 widening is evident and 15–20 changes from arrested hands to meal action and visible table arrangement. Food, fingers, utensils and setting carry a useful change from abstract round form to use. The contextual payoff survives; its onset is less clean than intended.

Slower second section: 2.4-second holds rather than 2-second holds are verifiable but only a 20% duration increase. Across still-image inspection I cannot certify that this feels like a slower return at playback. It also is not a literal reprise of the opening sequence: new images continue the same family, and the handheld reel echoes the projector reel rather than returning the initial clock. The roll section is returned to the side plate, the smaller morsel is lifted, then a reverse view shows the bread portions and water glass. This changes attention from a contour to an action and its setting. The main version makes this release much more legible than the control because the prior image is markedly tighter. The action itself is specific enough to carry the interval, independent of sound.

About 20.00–29.25: Round forms return more slowly through a telephone-like circular terminal assembly, iris, record label and hand-held film reel. These occupy the central region again, but with more context and perspective variation. The biological eye is a particularly effective change of material and implication after an electrical mechanism. Record and reel connect the round shape to use, even though these are held still rather than demonstrating operation. The hand-held reel is markedly elliptical; I read correspondence rather than exact contour registration. That is acceptable for an equivalent-radius idea but should not be described as perfect edge alignment.

About 29.50–32.00: The ball is held as another centrally prominent round form, with the nearby boy and curtain partially visible. About 32.25–34.25, wider context and changing actual frames reveal the boys; the ball rises/leaves the central zone and the foreground boy turns. This is an intelligible exit from shape correspondence into human action. Actual movement is visible in the dense samples. I cannot certify whether the motion speed is native, interpolated or retimed merely from these samples, so I only assert that the final passage contains source-image motion rather than a single unchanged picture.

Control comparison:
The unregistered control still communicates 'round things in archives.' It does not offer the same continuous spatial target: clock and circular aperture become huge, projector reel becomes relatively small and high, and later objects require gaze shifts and diameter readjustment. The control has more consistent image filling and fewer conspicuous black margins. Main pays for its stronger anchor with a small clock/aperture window, then much larger source windows; those frame-size changes are an additional visual rhythm. Despite that cost, the main version better supports the requested common-anchor construction. In particular the dinner release and final ball release have clearly larger before/after context changes. This is an observable difference, not a preference inferred from a transform ledger.

Largest gap / reservation:
Approximately 17.00–20.00, the nearly unchanged reverse dinner view after the morsel has been lifted. This is the least clearly necessary duration. The bread/plate/glass context is already established, the matched anchor has been released, and no new action redirects attention before the mechanical circular array arrives. The three seconds may provide an effective rest in playback, but the sampled images alone do not prove that need. This is the main reason I do not claim complete experiential acceptance.

Observable repair / falsifiable test:
Make a comparison that removes 1.0–1.5 seconds from the middle of the reverse dinner hold, preserving its entrance, enough time to see the torn bread, and its cut to the next round mechanism. Show both without the closing title. If viewers still describe the transition from rim to meal/action and then recognize the return to circular correspondence, while the shorter hold reduces a sense of waiting, shorten it. If they lose the distinction between geometric sequence and contextual interruption, retain the longer interval. Do not add another circle or explanatory text to solve this: the relevant variable is the aftermath duration.

Additional limits and strengths:
The early held images are near-static for roughly two seconds each; exact felt cadence and any contribution of the pulse track cannot be judged from contact sheets. The later sequence has longer holds, but its sense of a slower return is only provisionally supported until playback. The strongest visual achievements are stable central occupancy across unlike materials, the eye as a living counterpart to machines, and the two moves from constrained round form into contextual action. The final title is unnecessary for noticing those relationships. No required visual reconstruction is indicated by my inspection; test the dinner aftermath duration and audition the actual sound/picture before declaring a final audiovisual pass.

===== CRITIC_A_V01.txt =====
ALLWAYS 060 — CRITIC A / V01
Verdict: PASS for the narrow archival adaptation's visual operation: visible same-scene conduct makes the pickpocket accusation revisable, and the wide-opening comparison changes responsibility for the opening gestures. INCONCLUSIVE for the broader acceptance claim that the ending avoids fixed hero/victim roles. Full normal-speed/audiovisual acceptance is INCONCLUSIVE. This is not acceptance of the prescribed adult worktable proof.

Inspection scope and sequence: First inspected all five ordered restricted-opening sheets, then all five wide-opening sheets (whole70-second outputs, approximately1-second samples). Only afterward read prompt60.txt. Then examined36–48 at4fps,40–44.3 at8fps,54–66 at3fps from the actual restricted MP4. Finally inspected the original source around396–460 with ordered2fps frames, including the omitted426–430 interval and source internal crosscuts. No native normal-speed viewing or audio listening occurred. Source-range information was supplied after my first impressions; no build/ledger read.

First impression, before reading the prompt: A nervous-looking, isolated Chaplin is accused of pickpocketing. A marked earlier interval shows a distressed woman beside him, money in his own hands, his attention to her and a covert movement toward her pocket. Back in the encounter she discovers/reveals money and addresses the uniformed man. The officer's final hat gesture revises his stance. I read a mistaken accusation and exoneration. I did not initially read an unresolved social rearrangement: this is recognizably a comic good-deed/misrecognition resolution.

What restricted versus wide opening changes:
0–3 identifies archive/staged fiction. At3–12.6, restricted crop removes much of the uniformed man's body and restraining/holding arm. Chaplin's raised arm, leaning, fidgeting and loss of balance can look like his own suspicious or awkward conduct. Wide-opening version immediately shows the uniformed participant holding and positioning him. It makes the apparent awkwardness relational and the asymmetry of power visible. Neither version shows an actual theft in that interval. The subsequent source accusation card around12.6–16 supplies the alleged category, rather than the editor inventing new spoken testimony.
The restricted opening is the stronger experiment in revisable responsibility; wide is more immediately intelligible about coercion. This is a useful discriminating comparison, not evidence that the crop is neutral. The editorial removal of the officer is precisely what produces the misleading first impression.

Observable reversal and its limits:
16–18 “Moments earlier” explicitly marks chronology.
18–20 source card states mother lost her money.
20–approximately42.7: Chaplin produces/counts/handles money, repeatedly looks toward the woman, and attempts a covert gesture while she attends to her lap/companion. The money handling is prolonged; intent is partly carried by dramatic behavior and card context, not a newly explanatory narrator.
Approximately42.75–43.25: dense frames show his hand move from his held money toward the woman's near pocket/lap area and withdraw. The coins themselves are tiny and partly obscured. Giving/placing is a supported reading in conjunction with what follows; I do not claim a microscopic view proves each coin's direction.
Approximately43.9–45.5: uniformed observer in an original source crosscut. Source inspection confirms this is the same scene's observing figure, not a reaction borrowed from a separate event.
48–49 “Back at the encounter” marks return.
49–55: original encounter is now seen in the shared frame; restraint, defense and the woman's intervention coexist.
Approximately55–59: woman checks the money, looks down at it and becomes visibly animated; she then addresses the uniformed man, holding evidence in her hands. This corroborates the reinterpretation rather than leaving the label changed only by an editorial assertion.
Approximately63–65.7: officer changes demeanor, removes/tips hat and starts departing. This permits him to revise his judgment; he is not frozen forever as the accuser.
66–70 closes with source/adaptation card.

Responsibility, agency and reaction ownership:
Chaplin retains substantial agency: he handles and allocates his own money before being accused; he defends himself rather than only awaiting rescue. The woman's later check, display and address to the officer are her own visible actions in the shared shot. She changes the situation; she is not only an insert of suffering for Chaplin to redeem. The uniformed figure initially exercises physical control, then visibly changes his response. This is stronger than a montage of unrelated “bully/victim” expressions.
Source inspection matches the seated action, observer crosscut and later three-person encounter to the original scene. The film openly moves an earlier interval after the accusation and labels it. Within retained intervals the observed reactions remain in their source order. The four-second source gap426–430 includes further seated/woman/Chaplin movement before his encounter; omitting it simplifies the transition. I saw no independent evidence of a reaction falsely attached to a different event. I did not audit all output frames or certify source speech translation/authenticity as a separate research task.
The persistent staged-fiction/source label is visible in the inspected samples. It is small, but provenance is also introduced in opening and end cards. It should not be represented as a new documentary observation or contemporary performed experiment.

Largest remaining gap:
The final passage is conventional exoneration and gratitude. Chaplin is increasingly fixed as the generous innocent and the woman as grateful beneficiary. The officer's changed hat gesture complicates his identity, but the closing removal of the obstacle restores a neat comic moral arrangement. That is a partial, not complete answer to the prompt's instruction to avoid permanent hero/victim/bully roles and end on an unresolved continuing relationship. The source's theatrical star persona strengthens this reading. A good accusation-reversal experiment is not identical to the requested worktable mechanism in which the isolated participant chooses a position and initiates shared work with the obstructing person.

Observable repair condition:
Compare a version ending after the woman's evidence and the officer's first acknowledgement, while all three still share the frame, against the current officer departure at65–66. Retain enough of his changed response that he is not simply condemned. Test whether that stop leaves a reciprocal unresolved look rather than a completed vindication. Do not invent a new reaction or claim they continue a shared task when no such action is recorded. If the archive cannot show an ongoing negotiated relation, retain the film but label the acceptance scope “accusation reversal through archival reframing,” leaving the full non-type/worktable proof unfulfilled. This is a real scope limitation, not fixed by a new moralizing title.

Secondary concern/possible economy repair:
20–43 allocates over20seconds to fine money handling before the crucial hand/pocket gesture. My dense frames make the gesture recoverable, but I cannot certify real-time legibility from them. An experiment can shorten some redundant counting while preserving the initial ownership of the money, her visible distress, his decision, the actual placement, and the observer's source-linked response. Do not tighten so far that placing becomes indistinguishable from taking.

Strengths to protect:
The paired crops isolate an actual causal factor: another person's hold on the arm.
The chronology markers are candid.
The later woman's discovery is behavioral corroboration, not only a corrective caption.
The long shared frame49–66 gives woman, accused and officer room to act and respond together.
The officer is allowed to revise his stance.
The film is explicitly archival staged fiction, not fabricated documentary accusation.

Acceptance boundary: Narrow visual adaptation PASS; unresolved/non-type ending criterion INCONCLUSIVE; full chair/worktable proof NOT PERFORMED; normal-speed rhythm and sound/audiovisual acceptance INCONCLUSIVE. No comparable Losers reference footage was inspected, so “reference stronger” is not an available warranted verdict.

060 — INDEPENDENT CRITIC B, V01
Verdict: PASS for visual reframing of an accusation through same-scene context. INCONCLUSIVE for the stronger claim that viewers will see an unambiguous act of giving money. No full real-time audiovisual pass is claimed.

Method: Before consulting any construction information, inspected all five actual 1-second temporal sheets for IN_THE_FRAME_V01 and all five for WIDE_OPENING_V01, spanning 0–69 seconds. Then independently extracted and inspected the original supplied film over source396–460 seconds at2fps, in temporal order. No builder script, timeline, prompt text or other critic report consulted. No audio or native real-time playback. Whole-output inspection was at1fps, not every video frame; source broad audit at2fps and focused action at8fps. Times below are approximate to those sampling intervals, not certified edit-frame cut points. Historical plot knowledge is not treated as visible evidence.

First reading of actual output, before source audit:
At3 seconds the cropped woman sits with her head inclined and arms across her body/lap. At4–9 the man by the wall handles something at his coat/waist, makes sideways gestures and looks outward. At10–12 the encounter is dark and the other participant is heavily excluded. The accusation card13–15 makes suspicion explicit. This establishes an accusation much more firmly than it establishes a witnessed theft. That is a useful distinction: I never actually see him take something from the woman in the opening.

The flashback is clearly signposted at16–17. The loss card18–19 supplies missing context; the wider seated shot20–43 places the man beside the distressed woman, handling/counting paper-like money. His hands repeatedly work at his own lap and coat, and he looks toward her. After the observer shot44–45, the resumed seated action46–47 includes a low hand movement toward her side and withdrawal. Return48–49 is explicitly announced. The final wide encounter50–65 includes all three people, the woman looking at/handling money around56–59, then smiling and gesturing toward the man around60–63. The uniformed man disengages toward the end. I can read the accusation as premature, with her recognition and intervention affecting the encounter. The woman ceases to be merely an image of distress.

Money transfer: what the images support and do not support:
The long20–43 sequence is primarily money handling, not a clear gift. Its length does not by itself turn preparation into transfer. The brief low movement around47.0–47.5 is consistent with placing money at the woman's near-side clothing/lap, but black costume and overlapping hands obscure the small object and exact direction. It is not a clearly visible palm-to-palm handover. The later discovery/inspection56–59 and her positive response make giving a strong narrative inference. The edit therefore supports 'he apparently helped her, and the accusation is corrected' more strongly than 'we distinctly watched money leave his hand and enter her possession.' Do not describe the latter as verified visual fact.

Source audit and reaction assignment:
Original396–398 contains the loss card; around398–422 the same seated money sequence unfolds beside the distressed woman. Original422–423.5 cuts to the same uniformed observer, then returns to the seated pair. At424–426, densely inspected, the man manipulates the paper, lowers a hand toward the near side between them around425.0–425.6, withdraws, and resumes a relatively neutral pose. Original426.5–427.5 includes another observer view and the woman beginning to lift her head. Around428–430 he leans toward her, rises and leaves; she remains seated. Original430.5–439.5 contains wall gestures, the approach/physical encounter and accusation card. Original440–451.5 shows the woman joining, handling the paper and changing expression. Original452–458 contains her smiling/explanatory gestures and the uniformed man's departure.

The flashback therefore uses causal context from the SAME staged scene. I found no unrelated face imported to manufacture sympathy or hostility. The observer insert44–45 belongs to this money sequence in the original and is not an expression sourced from some unrelated event. The latter wide encounter is substantially continuous with the source's accusation aftermath. Her discovery and positive expression were not borrowed from a different scene. Source reordering is overtly signposted by 'Moments earlier' and 'Back at the encounter.' This is an honest temporal rearrangement at the level I audited.

The opening crop does remove the uniformed observer from the wall action and much of the physical encounter. The wide control reveals him earlier, making the man at the wall look more obviously watched/confronted. The main leaves more space for the viewer to attribute suspiciousness to isolated gestures. This changes available evidence but does not visibly fabricate a theft. The restored final wide image gives both the woman and the observer a chance to revise the situation. I see a comic misunderstanding rather than a permanent villain label. The crop-based opening is more effective for the intended reconsideration than the wide control, though the accusation card still does substantial interpretive work in both.

Largest gap / pacing reservation:
Approximately20–43: almost24 seconds of seated counting, checking and repeated small money gestures before the short consequential movement. This preserves source continuity but attention may drift before the action that matters. The woman remains head-down for much of it. The late recognition restores agency, yet the preparatory stretch risks reinstating passive-sufferer versus benevolent-man categories simply through unequal action time. This is a pacing/attention reservation from sampled images, not proof that real-time viewing fails.

Observable repair / test:
Preserve one continuous wide passage that includes money visibly in his hand, the low action toward her side, withdrawal, and her subsequent movement/discovery from original424–430. The existing cut after main47–48 loses the immediate aftermath that would help interpret direction. Use some of that source continuation before the return card; remove redundant earlier counting time so duration need not grow. Keep the observer in his actual original relative position rather than inserting him as a moral judgment after a newly reordered action. Then ask fresh viewers, without a synopsis, what changed possession and what the woman herself does to resolve the encounter. If they still report only counting or cannot identify her intervention, giving/agency remains unproven. An explanatory 'he gave her money' card would assert the answer rather than repair the evidence.

Strengths and delivery limits:
The persistent staged-fiction/source label is visible throughout the sampled image area and title/end cards identify an archive re-edit. The main does not present itself as newly performed work or documentary evidence. Legibility of the small footer on a phone still needs actual display testing. The source staging, low-resolution cash/hand overlap and intertitles make this an archival adaptation, with understandable limits on fine action clarity. Within those limits, same-scene context, temporal signposting, the woman's later active response and inclusion of all three at the end achieve a defensible visual reframing. Native pace, comfortable reading time and any accidental sound are outside this image-only review.

Actual revised ending:
At52–53 the woman smiles while holding the paper/money. Around53.25–55 she turns toward the central man, raises a hand and gestures while looking between the men. Around55–56.75 all three remain visible; the uniformed man stays at the left with his hand near/on the central man's shoulder. The final cut at57 comes before the departure seen in V01. This preserves the woman's active recognition and intervention while removing the most complete comic dismissal of the accusation. It ends in a shared negotiation/acknowledgment rather than the authority figure walking away defeated. The woman still smiles, so this does not create radical moral ambiguity; it adequately avoids a final image of a permanent villain expelled from the scene.

Control comparison:
Wide-opening V02 still shows the uniformed observer during the early wall gestures, so the central man's gestures are contextualized earlier as watched/confronted behavior. Restricted main still withholds much of that relationship until the flashback and final wide scene. The main is stronger for the requested change in reading; both preserve the same later evidence and the woman's agency. The accusation card13–15 does substantial interpretive work in both. Neither opening visibly proves a theft.

Reaction/source integrity:
The restored observer and woman's first movement match the source's local order after money handling; no new reaction from another event was introduced. The main26-second ellipsis changes duration within the same seated setup rather than attributing a foreign expression. 'Moments earlier'16–17 and 'Back at the encounter'42 preserve overt temporal signposting. Final wide action is consistent with the source encounter I audited. The persistent staged-fiction/archive label remains visible on sampled frames and the cards do not claim a newly performed scene.

Largest remaining gap:
Approximately26–36, ten seconds of small money sorting/looking movements before the observer insert and low action. This is still the least advancing section. It is substantially less disproportionate than V01's20–43 stretch and no longer blocks my visual pass. Whether all ten seconds earn their attention is a native-playback question. Further arbitrary cutting risks losing the contrast between his deliberation and the observer's incomplete view.

Optional falsifiable refinement:

If inference survives and anticipation improves, keep the tighter version; if the low action becomes abrupt or the observer seems unmotivated, retain V02. No explanatory giving card is needed.

ALLWAYS 060 — CRITIC A / V01
Verdict: PASS for the narrow archival adaptation's visual operation: visible same-scene conduct makes the pickpocket accusation revisable, and the wide-opening comparison changes responsibility for the opening gestures. INCONCLUSIVE for the broader acceptance claim that the ending avoids fixed hero/victim roles. Full normal-speed/audiovisual acceptance is INCONCLUSIVE. Inspection scope and sequence: First inspected all five ordered restricted-opening sheets, then all five wide-opening sheets (whole70-second outputs, approximately1-second samples). First impression, before reading the prompt: A nervous-looking, isolated Chaplin is accused of pickpocketing. A marked earlier interval shows a distressed woman beside him, money in his own hands, his attention to her and a covert movement toward her pocket. Back in the encounter she discovers/reveals money and addresses the uniformed man. The officer's final hat gesture revises his stance. I read a mistaken accusation and exoneration. I did not initially read an unresolved social rearrangement: this is recognizably a comic good-deed/misrecognition resolution.

What restricted versus wide opening changes:
0–3 identifies archive/staged fiction. At3–12.6, restricted crop removes much of the uniformed man's body and restraining/holding arm. Chaplin's raised arm, leaning, fidgeting and loss of balance can look like his own suspicious or awkward conduct. Wide-opening version immediately shows the uniformed participant holding and positioning him. It makes the apparent awkwardness relational and the asymmetry of power visible. Neither version shows an actual theft in that interval. The subsequent source accusation card around12.6–16 supplies the alleged category, rather than the editor inventing new spoken testimony.
The restricted opening is the stronger experiment in revisable responsibility; wide is more immediately intelligible about coercion. This is a useful discriminating comparison, not evidence that the crop is neutral. The editorial removal of the officer is precisely what produces the misleading first impression.

Observable reversal and its limits:
16–18 “Moments earlier” explicitly marks chronology.
18–20 source card states mother lost her money.
20–approximately42.7: Chaplin produces/counts/handles money, repeatedly looks toward the woman, and attempts a covert gesture while she attends to her lap/companion. The money handling is prolonged; intent is partly carried by dramatic behavior and card context, not a newly explanatory narrator.
Approximately42.75–43.25: dense frames show his hand move from his held money toward the woman's near pocket/lap area and withdraw. The coins themselves are tiny and partly obscured. Giving/placing is a supported reading in conjunction with what follows; I do not claim a microscopic view proves each coin's direction.
Approximately43.9–45.5: uniformed observer in an original source crosscut. Source inspection confirms this is the same scene's observing figure, not a reaction borrowed from a separate event.

49–55: original encounter is now seen in the shared frame; restraint, defense and the woman's intervention coexist.

Approximately63–65.7: officer changes demeanor, removes/tips hat and starts departing. This permits him to revise his judgment; he is not frozen forever as the accuser.
66–70 closes with source/adaptation card.

Responsibility, agency and reaction ownership:
Chaplin retains substantial agency: he handles and allocates his own money before being accused; he defends himself rather than only awaiting rescue. The woman's later check, display and address to the officer are her own visible actions in the shared shot. She changes the situation; she is not only an insert of suffering for Chaplin to redeem. The uniformed figure initially exercises physical control, then visibly changes his response. This is stronger than a montage of unrelated “bully/victim” expressions.
Source inspection matches the seated action, observer crosscut and later three-person encounter to the original scene. The film openly moves an earlier interval after the accusation and labels it. Within retained intervals the observed reactions remain in their source order. The four-second source gap426–430 includes further seated/woman/Chaplin movement before his encounter; omitting it simplifies the transition. I saw no independent evidence of a reaction falsely attached to a different event. I did not audit all output frames or certify source speech translation/authenticity as a separate research task.
The persistent staged-fiction/source label is visible in the inspected samples. It is small, but provenance is also introduced in opening and end cards. It should not be represented as a new documentary observation or contemporary performed experiment.

Largest remaining gap:
The final passage is conventional exoneration and gratitude. Chaplin is increasingly fixed as the generous innocent and the woman as grateful beneficiary. The officer's changed hat gesture complicates his identity, but the closing removal of the obstacle restores a neat comic moral arrangement. That is a partial, not complete answer to the prompt's instruction to avoid permanent hero/victim/bully roles and end on an unresolved continuing relationship. The source's theatrical star persona strengthens this reading. A good accusation-reversal experiment is not identical to the requested worktable mechanism in which the isolated participant chooses a position and initiates shared work with the obstructing person.

Observable repair condition:

Do not invent a new reaction or claim they continue a shared task when no such action is recorded. If the archive cannot show an ongoing negotiated relation, retain the film but label the acceptance scope “accusation reversal through archival reframing,” leaving the full non-type/worktable proof unfulfilled. This is a real scope limitation, not fixed by a new moralizing title.

An experiment can shorten some redundant counting while preserving the initial ownership of the money, her visible distress, his decision, the actual placement, and the observer's source-linked response. Do not tighten so far that placing becomes indistinguishable from taking.

Strengths to protect:

The chronology markers are candid.
The later woman's discovery is behavioral corroboration, not only a corrective caption.
The long shared frame49–66 gives woman, accused and officer room to act and respond together.

The film is explicitly archival staged fiction, not fabricated documentary accusation.
