It is a second sample/task instance, not the original carton mysteriously restored. This candor protects identity, while sacrificing the prompt's same-original-sheet retest.

Analogy protection:
17–19 card says the next shot is a different material and not the same sample. Source footers distinguish folding/time study, staged advertising wetting and industrial cutting. Final plate37–45 says different sources/no common recovery test and separate samples/held frames. Together these are enough to prevent a reasonable one-sample reading. The metal image follows a darkened textured edge, but the film explicitly makes resemblance an editorial association. I see no unmarked simulation or claim that wet paper physically becomes metal. “Matter” remains provisional in the opening; no reference film's formal method is verified here.

Main versus held-endpoint control:
Control freezes the box, the final darkened paper frame and a metal curl across the same broad durations. It permits comparison of appearances, but cannot show which actions produced them. For example12–17 control already has a large dark patch throughout; in main,13.4–16.5 establishes growth after contact. At19–29 control a curl is simply present; in main its generation and severing are visible. Main therefore wins decisively for observable response. This comparison is informative but easy: holding terminal images intentionally removes causal evidence. It does not itself prove a subtler analogy is artistically effective at normal speed.

Final comparison,37–45:
Labels are legible at full frame: FOLDED / DARKENED / CURLED–SEVERED. It places three outcomes side by side and changes the classification of the first shot from industrial productivity to one of several ways matter is acted upon. That is a modest conceptual reframe. It chiefly catalogs results already understood, however; it introduces no new observation about the opening material's behavior or recovery. Strong claim that the ending changes how the opening action is understood remains INCONCLUSIVE.
The “Recorded endpoints” title is potentially stronger than the images warrant. Carton panel still has hands engaged; metal panel shows a chip in a continuing process, not a verified unloaded aftermath; the paper panel includes the source advertising slogan. These are endpoints of selected intervals, not necessarily endpoints of the physical changes. The explicit no-common-recovery-test line limits the overclaim, but the ambiguity remains.

Largest remaining gap:
A film titled What Stays can invite a memory/residue question, yet it supplies selected states without a common recovery interval or comparable before/after test. The film is honest about this, so it is not making a demonstrated false physics claim. But honesty about absent evidence is not evidence that the larger material-memory proposition or transformed-return criterion has been achieved.

Falsifiable repair options:
For the current archive scope, replace “Recorded endpoints” with “Selected recorded states” if retaining active-operation frames. This is a wording correction, not a perceptual cure.
For a stronger final reframe, compare each initial state with its last actually observed state (before/action/selected end frame, with sample identity retained). Ask an unprompted viewer to identify a new distinction about the opening carton after that plate; if they only repeat “it was folded,” the ending still catalogs rather than reframes. If showing recovery is essential, obtain an actual same-sample unloading/return observation. Do not call reverse playback, another carton or an earlier freeze recovery. Do not imply the advertising patch has dried or is permanent when no such interval is recorded.

Strengths to preserve:
Wetting contact and spreading are especially readable.
Machine curl generation and separation carry a real transformation rather than decorative texture.
Uniform framing avoids shape distortions.
Explicit analogy/sample/source labels prevent a misleading continuous-specimen story.
Another-carton label prevents a false reset.
No-common-recovery-test qualification is appropriately specific.
The main/control pair demonstrates exactly what visible action contributes beyond an endpoint picture.

Acceptance boundary: THREE DISTINCT VISIBLE RESPONSES PASS. CONSTRUCTED ANALOGY HONESTY PASS. MAIN STRONGER THAN ENDPOINT CONTROL. FINAL TRANSFORMATIVE REFRAME INCONCLUSIVE. The substantive repair works. The actual plate now has three earlier/later rows, not three supposedly stable end products. The distracting advertising headline is absent from the chosen later frame. The cutting row shows the tall attached spiral on the left, then the cleared central area and smaller cut pieces on the right after the long ribbon's departure. The carton row links back to the opening operator,clock/task and shows changed positions of tabs/walls between selected source27.5 and36.0seconds. Nothing on this actual plate establishes permanence,elastic recovery or comparative absorbency rates. Its function is to compare visible changes and open a question. That is honest and useful.

Largest remaining defect in V02: the 'Earlier view' and 'Later view' headings are too far left. At native resolution the first starts over the row-label gutter and the second starts over the left image column, while the pictures occupy columns starting around190 and575pixels. No picture substitution is needed for this repair.

I would not demand the previously suggested4–5second duration without native playback: the revised information density has changed. Once headings are aligned, use actual playback to check that viewers can compare all three rows and read the qualification comfortably. The remaining weak carton comparison can be tested by asking what physical change each pair shows; if viewers report only a rotated box, choose states with more visible wall/tab angle difference or rely explicitly on the earlier moving action.

Disposition: substantive visual repair accepted, with V02 heading alignment requiring correction. Core cause/response pass retained. The plate is a clearer comparison of recorded states and avoids unsupported end-state claims. Real-time rhythm,audio and recovery conclusions remain outside this inspection.

Verdict: qualified VISUAL PASS for the archival adaptation's core operation: a human connected to an air-measuring chamber, a chamber response visible within an uninterrupted shot, an explicitly different mechanical demonstration, and a final depleted shape. Method and independence
I first inspected all five ordered one-second main-film sheets before consulting the prompt or source material. I then extracted and inspected the actual main's human passage at four frames/second (3–19 seconds), and mechanical passage at two frames/second (21–56 seconds). I enlarged actual main frames at 13, 16.5 and 32.6 seconds. After the initial viewing I read the relevant prompt63.txt requirements and inspected sparse whole-source overviews, human-source extracts around 466–490 seconds at two frames/second, and mechanical-source extracts around 450–475 and 500–550 seconds at one frame/second. Finally I inspected all eight ordered one-second sheets of the actual 119.5-second ORIGINAL_SCENE_TIMING_V01 comparison. I did not inspect builder scripts, a ledger or another critic's report. The parent supplied source ranges after my initial independent viewing; the visible source frames, rather than that explanation, support the findings below. No audio audition, native real-time playback, target-display evaluation or physiological measurement occurred.

What changes, and what actually causes the reading
3–10.5 seconds: the dark-suited man holds a mouthpiece connected to the chamber by a hose. The light band in the chamber changes height modestly relative to its fixed supports. This is a modest visual event; a still image alone would not establish it.

Approximately 10.5–12 seconds: a source dissolve replaces that man with a light-shirted demonstrator. The concurrent reset/change of the chamber is NOT evidence of a breath moving its contents. This transition should never be offered as the causal proof.

Approximately 12–15 seconds: after the dissolve has resolved, the light-shirted man remains attached to the mouthpiece and the chamber band rises and falls against fixed rods. The motion is present in the original source within that same take. That is the decisive evidence. The exact inhalation/exhalation phase and the apparatus's internal mechanism cannot be diagnosed from these frames, but a live breathing demonstration coupled to a moving air-volume indicator is visually supported. This does not rest on an actor substitution or an unrelated moving object.

15–19 seconds: the face is removed and the chamber fills the narrow central crop. Its moving band remains legible. This crop works because the preceding continuous view has already shown the human, mouthpiece and apparatus together. It is an effect-only continuation, not independent proof of the cause. The central strip with black sides is visually austere but directs attention successfully.

19–21 seconds: the explicit 'A DIFFERENT CAUSE / Engine intake / mechanical air' card correctly breaks any claim that the following bag is human respiration. The actual car, hose connection and bonnet apparatus reinforce that distinction.

21–approximately 29.5 seconds: wide preparation, a coupling close-up and the expanding bag establish a separate practical demonstration. The material grows rounder and tauter against its wire hoop. The exact motor plumbing is not fully traceable in the crop; the image and explicit caption together establish the mechanical reading.

Approximately 29.5–33 seconds: the bag is a full, rounded sphere. Around the transition at 33 seconds there is a very brief laboratory/apparatus insert and then a different car view with the bag already partly depleted. This is an interval and shot change, not a continuous observation of full-to-partial emptying.

33–49.5 seconds: within the head-on and subsequent side passages, the bag does continue becoming narrower while the car moves. The fixed hoop makes the loss of fullness readable even as framing changes. These local changes provide more than a before/after juxtaposition, though they do not repair the omitted interval from the full sphere.

Approximately 49.5–56 seconds: a gauge insert, then the limp wrinkled bag and a wider car view, give the demonstration an aftermath. The hoop preserves a concrete memory of the earlier round volume. This is depletion during/after mechanical use; it should not be described as quiet human recovery or as a fan having stopped. The human chamber is not revisited at the end.

Largest remaining gap and observable repair
The largest causal/editorial gap is approximately 32.8–33.5 seconds: a full stationary bag gives way to an already partly depleted bag on a moving car, with a fleeting laboratory image between them. A private ledger cannot make that ellipsis legible to the viewer. It does not invalidate the later within-shot depletion, but it can make the total loss seem more continuously observed than it is.

A falsifiable repair would remove the fleeting laboratory fragment and mark this join with a brief 'Later in the same demonstration' caption, or retain a short original-source car departure passage showing the bag full as the vehicle begins moving. Check the revised film with the ledger hidden: the viewer should identify an elapsed interval rather than report having watched the entire sphere collapse continuously. Do not add a claim that a source dissolve is a physical air event. This is a clarity improvement, not grounds to fabricate missing physical continuity.

Timing comparison
The original-scene comparison strengthens source accountability. Its human section keeps the face present through 19 seconds, confirming that the main's 15–19 crop is taken from a live connected demonstration. Its mechanical section restores the prolonged laboratory procedure (roughly 30–60 seconds), the full bag and vehicle preparation (roughly 61–73), the departure and early diminution (roughly 74–82), and later depletion (roughly 86–109), followed by the nearly empty car (109–114). It reveals what the main has omitted and makes the causal chronology easier to audit. It is also strongly dominated by a laboratory procedure whose fine purpose is difficult to infer silently; it delays the human/mechanical comparison substantially. The main is the more focused short, provided the interval at 33 seconds remains honestly qualified. The comparison is a useful audit, not a replacement I would prefer for the intended concise encounter.

Strengths and scope
The strongest construction is the change from person-plus-apparatus to apparatus alone, followed by a clearly labeled new cause. The final limp membrane inside its unchanged hoop makes depleted air volume unusually specific and memorable. The absence of a participation demand is explicit in the opening and persistent footer; the image sequence does not itself instruct a breathing cycle.

The human source is an anatomical/educational breathing demonstration, not incidental chest movement recruited as evidence. The mechanical source is a staged industrial demonstration from a Chevrolet/General Motors promotional film; that advertising context matters. Neither source licenses a medical claim or an equation between a human lung and the car's bag. The film passes as a carefully bounded archival analogy about visible air-volume effects. It does not satisfy the original prompt's literal new-scene production requirements, and its final quiet is material depletion rather than documented recovery of a breathing person.

The largest remaining gap is the angle/time jump at 38 seconds from the fuller moving bag to a partly emptied one. It is now a much smaller causal objection because the fuller bag has already been seen in use. A brief 'Later in the same demonstration' marker at this join would make the omitted interval explicit if the film needs that degree of evidence clarity. Remove the approximately quarter-second laboratory fragment just before 33 seconds as a separate polish: it is too brief to explain anything and briefly redirects attention to a different apparatus. A reviewer should be able to describe a bag losing fullness during selected recorded stages, rather than report an uninterrupted collapse. No new physical event needs to be invented.

Strength and remaining scope
V02 better binds the initial sphere, moving car and eventual wrinkled membrane into one specific material event. The added START view gives the transition a practical beginning. The source-context limits remain: anatomical/educational breathing footage contrasted with staged Chevrolet/General Motors industrial promotion; an analogy between effects, not an equation of human physiology and a car's air supply. Method and independence
I first inspected all five ordered one-second main-film sheets before consulting the prompt or source material. I then extracted and inspected the actual main's human passage at four frames/second (3–19 seconds), and mechanical passage at two frames/second (21–56 seconds). I enlarged actual main frames at 13, 16.5 and 32.6 seconds. After the initial viewing I read the relevant prompt63.txt requirements and inspected sparse whole-source overviews, human-source extracts around 466–490 seconds at two frames/second, and mechanical-source extracts around 450–475 and 500–550 seconds at one frame/second. Finally I inspected all eight ordered one-second sheets of the actual 119.5-second ORIGINAL_SCENE_TIMING_V01 comparison. I did not inspect builder scripts, a ledger or another critic's report. The parent supplied source ranges after my initial independent viewing; the visible source frames, rather than that explanation, support the findings below. No audio audition, native real-time playback, target-display evaluation or physiological measurement occurred.

What changes, and what actually causes the reading
3–10.5 seconds: the dark-suited man holds a mouthpiece connected to the chamber by a hose. The light band in the chamber changes height modestly relative to its fixed supports. This is a modest visual event; a still image alone would not establish it.

Approximately 10.5–12 seconds: a source dissolve replaces that man with a light-shirted demonstrator. The concurrent reset/change of the chamber is NOT evidence of a breath moving its contents. This transition should never be offered as the causal proof.

Approximately 12–15 seconds: after the dissolve has resolved, the light-shirted man remains attached to the mouthpiece and the chamber band rises and falls against fixed rods. The motion is present in the original source within that same take. That is the decisive evidence. The exact inhalation/exhalation phase and the apparatus's internal mechanism cannot be diagnosed from these frames, but a live breathing demonstration coupled to a moving air-volume indicator is visually supported. This does not rest on an actor substitution or an unrelated moving object.

15–19 seconds: the face is removed and the chamber fills the narrow central crop. Its moving band remains legible. This crop works because the preceding continuous view has already shown the human, mouthpiece and apparatus together. It is an effect-only continuation, not independent proof of the cause. The central strip with black sides is visually austere but directs attention successfully.

19–21 seconds: the explicit 'A DIFFERENT CAUSE / Engine intake / mechanical air' card correctly breaks any claim that the following bag is human respiration. The actual car, hose connection and bonnet apparatus reinforce that distinction.

21–approximately 29.5 seconds: wide preparation, a coupling close-up and the expanding bag establish a separate practical demonstration. The material grows rounder and tauter against its wire hoop. The exact motor plumbing is not fully traceable in the crop; the image and explicit caption together establish the mechanical reading.

Approximately 29.5–33 seconds: the bag is a full, rounded sphere. Around the transition at 33 seconds there is a very brief laboratory/apparatus insert and then a different car view with the bag already partly depleted. This is an interval and shot change, not a continuous observation of full-to-partial emptying.

33–49.5 seconds: within the head-on and subsequent side passages, the bag does continue becoming narrower while the car moves. The fixed hoop makes the loss of fullness readable even as framing changes. These local changes provide more than a before/after juxtaposition, though they do not repair the omitted interval from the full sphere.

Approximately 49.5–56 seconds: a gauge insert, then the limp wrinkled bag and a wider car view, give the demonstration an aftermath. The hoop preserves a concrete memory of the earlier round volume. This is depletion during/after mechanical use; it should not be described as quiet human recovery or as a fan having stopped. The human chamber is not revisited at the end.

Largest remaining gap and observable repair
The largest causal/editorial gap is approximately 32.8–33.5 seconds: a full stationary bag gives way to an already partly depleted bag on a moving car, with a fleeting laboratory image between them. A private ledger cannot make that ellipsis legible to the viewer. It does not invalidate the later within-shot depletion, but it can make the total loss seem more continuously observed than it is.

A falsifiable repair would remove the fleeting laboratory fragment and mark this join with a brief 'Later in the same demonstration' caption, or retain a short original-source car departure passage showing the bag full as the vehicle begins moving. Check the revised film with the ledger hidden: the viewer should identify an elapsed interval rather than report having watched the entire sphere collapse continuously. Do not add a claim that a source dissolve is a physical air event. This is a clarity improvement, not grounds to fabricate missing physical continuity.

Timing comparison
The original-scene comparison strengthens source accountability. Its human section keeps the face present through 19 seconds, confirming that the main's 15–19 crop is taken from a live connected demonstration. Its mechanical section restores the prolonged laboratory procedure (roughly 30–60 seconds), the full bag and vehicle preparation (roughly 61–73), the departure and early diminution (roughly 74–82), and later depletion (roughly 86–109), followed by the nearly empty car (109–114). It reveals what the main has omitted and makes the causal chronology easier to audit. It is also strongly dominated by a laboratory procedure whose fine purpose is difficult to infer silently; it delays the human/mechanical comparison substantially. The main is the more focused short, provided the interval at 33 seconds remains honestly qualified. The comparison is a useful audit, not a replacement I would prefer for the intended concise encounter.

Strengths and scope
The strongest construction is the change from person-plus-apparatus to apparatus alone, followed by a clearly labeled new cause. The final limp membrane inside its unchanged hoop makes depleted air volume unusually specific and memorable. The absence of a participation demand is explicit in the opening and persistent footer; the image sequence does not itself instruct a breathing cycle.

The human source is an anatomical/educational breathing demonstration, not incidental chest movement recruited as evidence. The mechanical source is a staged industrial demonstration from a Chevrolet/General Motors promotional film; that advertising context matters. Neither source licenses a medical claim or an equation between a human lung and the car's bag. The film passes as a carefully bounded archival analogy about visible air-volume effects. It does not satisfy the original prompt's literal new-scene production requirements, and its final quiet is material depletion rather than documented recovery of a breathing person.

Verdict: qualified VISUAL PASS for the archival comparative essay. The film makes a useful distinction between similar projected curves, a screen-space movement record, and the different circumstances producing them. Full normal-speed audiovisual acceptance remains INCONCLUSIVE. I cannot return 'reference stronger': I have not inspected a comparable reference film. Method
I first inspected all four actual ordered one-second temporal sheets before reading FITS.json, prompt.txt or timing records. I then enlarged actual main frames at 26, 32 and 38 seconds, inspected the three supplied source-reference images and diver_track.jpg, and extracted the actual movie's 10–24-second action passage at four frames/second. I read FITS.json, prompt.txt and TIMELINE_SOUND.json after the initial film inspection. I independently recomputed the quadratic and trajectory RMS residuals from stored samples. No audio audition, native real-time playback or target-display inspection occurred. Source auditing here means selected supplied reference images and sampled action in the actual export, not a complete viewing of each original source film. No builder script or other critic report was read.

Actual visual development
0–2 seconds: the title establishes a question about cause without immediately showing a formula.

2–10 seconds: the suspended stage drapery offers two readily visible concave outlines while dancers move underneath. The visible suspension points and static fabric provide construction context; the specific support forces remain unknown. This is already a distinct static outline, rather than a trajectory. Its shared curve is noticeable, though the busy stage and distant view compete for attention.

10–16 seconds: the diver leaves a platform, turns in the air, descends and enters the water. The platform's changing position and the background moving into view make the following camera observable. The action has consequence and completion. A clean geometrical flight arc is difficult to perceive because the camera keeps the changing body near the frame center. That difficulty becomes legitimate evidence later; it should not be disguised as a measured ballistic path.

16–24 seconds: a tool contacts material and curling strips rise, separate and fall. The coil is visibly produced during cutting, not merely found as an attractive texture. This is the clearest different cause in the film. The curl returning over itself also gives a genuine reason for a single-valued quadratic to fail.

24–26 seconds: 'THREE CURVES. DIFFERENT CAUSES' explicitly announces the comparison and says that guides are hypotheses, with no physical law measured. That protects the historical observations from becoming a false universal-form claim.

26–32 seconds: the two curtain guides sit closely on the sampled lower edge of the left fabric swag. The static-outline label and unresolved-cause wording are correct. At actual output size the models nearly coincide; this is a useful demonstration that two close fits cannot identify the generating cause, rather than a meaningful visual distinction between those curve families. Preserve that modest claim.

32–38 seconds: numbered body-center samples surround a frozen diver picture and the amber time-parametric fit misses several blue points. The caption correctly calls this SCREEN TRACE, says the camera follows, and explicitly excludes a world-space flight path. The points represent successive frames; they must not be read as points along the silhouette of this one body. The single frozen curled pose does create that perceptual risk despite the correct wording.

38–44 seconds: blue points follow a section of the projected coil that turns back in x; the amber quadratic instead bridges across it and visibly departs. The 'projected outline' label and 'neither is a flight path' limitation distinguish this from the preceding temporal trace. Here the mismatch teaches something concrete about the model's representational limit. It does not look like a merely misplaced guide once the original coil and blue samples are visible.

44–56 seconds: all guides vanish and the dancers continue moving while the drapery stays supported. The return activates a specific earlier observation: people can change position under a curved outline whose suspension is unchanged. It is a good return, although the final seconds widen/reframe the stage rather than reveal a release of the fabric. Do not claim the prompt's gently released chain has been reproduced.

Geometry and source-sample checks
Visual alignment: the curtain dots coincide with the selected lower swag boundary on curtain_reference.png and actual frame 26; the coil dots lie on the chosen upper curling section on steel_reference.png and actual frame 38. The marked diver sample cloud occupies the screen positions illustrated across diver_track.jpg, rather than tracing the visible contour of diver_reference.png. I found no gross crop/coordinate displacement in the actual overlays. This was visual verification with finite source resolution and the stated manual-sampling uncertainty, not a new high-precision contour extraction.

Numerical check: using the stored sample coordinates and coefficients gives curtain quadratic vertical RMS 1.1856 px, steel quadratic vertical RMS 36.2297 px, and diver time-parametric 2D RMS 25.2728 px, matching FITS.json. These are different residual definitions and should not be ranked as equivalent measures of physical truth. The catenary-family residual is recorded as 1.7414 px; I did not independently reconstruct that family parameterization. The source records describe the drapery as decorative fabric under unknown supports/perspective, the diver samples as unregistered screen positions, and steel as projected coil outline. Those limits match the actual captions.

Largest remaining gap and observable repair condition
The weakest link is 32–38 seconds, prepared by 10–16: a temporal screen trace is demonstrated on one held body image. This makes the distinction correct in text but less direct in the pictures. The original following-camera shot also makes the pre-overlay family resemblance weaker than the curtain/coil resemblance.

A concrete repair would replace part of the six-second diver still with the same source passage replayed, advancing one numbered body-center marker at its own source time, then retain the completed cloud and failed fit for the final two seconds. Keep the frame unregistered and its 'not world-space' label. A reviewer with FITS.json hidden should then be able to state that the blue dots record different times, not the diver's outline, and point to the changing platform/background as a source of screen-space ambiguity. Do not invent a smooth trajectory or stabilize away the camera motion merely to force a prettier match. This refinement would strengthen temporal legibility; the present captioned construction passes narrowly as an explicit model comparison.

Strengths to protect and limits
Protect the substantial observation-first passage through 24 seconds, the cutting event's visible production and separation, the coil's conspicuous model failure, and the unannotated stage return. The film is more specific than a catalogue of curved objects because it differentiates suspension, flight recorded by a moving camera, and tool-produced curling. It also allows a close fit to remain causally uninformative. The six-second annotation holds are comparatively uniform and diagrammatic, but the failed coil fit earns its time. The source soundtrack is supplied and documented as unauditioned; image-based criticism cannot certify contact synchronization, sound rhythm or the complete watchable audiovisual result.

What the five records actually preserve
A, 4–12 seconds: a woman repeatedly brings a fine tool/brush toward the cup and reaches back toward her materials. B, 12–16.5 seconds: a worker presses material against the machine and sparks appear, then diminish as contact changes. That visible response makes the practical action specific. C, 16.5–28.5 seconds: an overhead view of drawing equipment changes to a seated designer seen from the side. D, 28.5–40.5 seconds: a reclining woman takes up the red vessel, tips it over a cup, returns it upright and holds the cup near her face. E, 40.5–52.5 seconds: the opening office man dissolves into a woman at another desk arrangement. 75–95 seconds is the largest remaining formal gap. Five equal four-second held final frames create a catalogue of endpoints; A, B, C and D have not all finished their practices. The ending's strongest change of understanding is therefore narrow: some work leaves the frame, while other work is simply arrested by the editor. This is a useful distinction but weaker in the images than in the explanatory labels.

The current clear freeze labels should be preserved if any freeze remains. This is a form improvement within an already bounded visual pass, not evidence that missing participant testimony can be repaired by timing.

Method

Actual grammar visible in the export
0–9.6 seconds: eight large spare symbols are individually introduced with names: GATE, FORK, LOOP, LADDER, CROSS, BOWL, SPARK, FIELD. The symbols are easy to distinguish in still inspection. They are an invented eight-glyph vocabulary, not a displayed A–Z alphabet. Spoken naming cannot be assessed here.

9.6–14.4 seconds: all eight appear again in the same order, at 0.6 seconds each. This is the only rapid complete all-symbol cycle before replacement starts; the earlier slower introduction supplies the other exposure. Whether that is enough to teach eight positions in real time remains untested.

First image replacements occur at 14.4 (carton folding), 19.8 (curling metal), 25.2 (cup painting), 30.6 (projector), 36.0 (diver), 41.4 (reclining pour), 46.8 (office), and 52.2 seconds (people with placards). Each persists in its own serial position after substitution. The actual slot-center sheets make this progression visible without relying solely on the ledger: later rows contain increasingly more pictures at their former symbol positions; no replaced position goes back to its symbol.

These are advancing excerpts, not the identical six-tenths of a second repeated. The carton worker's hands change position; the metal curl rises and falls out of view; the painter reaches and returns; the projector's material moves; the diver approaches departure and becomes airborne. The office changes occupants through a source dissolve across its small number of appearances. That is a change within the source, not evidence of one person's continuous action. The placard scene has only two image appearances and the least time to develop. The film is properly a grammar of records, not eight equally complete events.

The arbitrary symbol-to-record associations do not have to be semantic illustrations. 'FORK' is not a claim that the tool in the metal shot is a fork, and 'SPARK' does not describe the office record. Their meaningful constraint is persistent position and identity. Avoid describing these as eight words proved by matching objects.

At 48–57.6 seconds all slots are images for two complete cycles. This is the most demanding passage for remembering the vanished symbols and the clearest separation from a generic collection of attractive symbols intercut with footage. The symbols have actually disappeared, while the image order remains.

At 57.6 seconds the carton record continues beyond its customary slot. Through 67.6 seconds it has time for several operations: manipulation at the carton edge, closing/flipping the box, reopening and further arranging another box. It is the first record introduced and the one given the longest cumulative exposure before this release, so returning to it is well prepared. 'The action leaves the slot' means the fixed temporal slot is abandoned; neither the man nor box has to leave the image frame. The passage remains a repetitive demonstration, not a terminal completed task. At 67.6–70.6 the image is explicitly held and labeled 'HELD FINAL FRAME / Editorial hold after the released passage'. That endpoint is editorial, not a person observed stopping.

Ordered versus shuffled actual pictures
The control changes the order of identities within each cycle. Even before many substitutions, symbols shift relative to their neighbors. After replacement, the carton, metal, pottery and projector positions migrate while keeping their own record identities and advancing source action. Thus the ordered version supports the specific anticipation 'the next place should be X'; the control chiefly supports recognizing recurring content and declining symbol count. Both versions visibly perform substitution, and both share the same carton release. The control therefore tests the value of stable order, not whether images alone are sufficient or whether the ending works. I prefer the ordered version for the intended serial constraint. This is an inspection of available structure, not evidence that an audience performed better on a prediction task.

Diagnostics and their limits
GRAMMAR.json records 30fps, 18-frame slots, ten eight-slot cycles from 9.6–57.6, monotonic replacement and progressive source starts. Its first-replacement times agree with the actual slot sequence inspected. GRAMMAR_CHECKS.json reports passing duration/order/progression invariants and correctly says viewer_learning 'not measured'. Those structural reports do not prove real-time legibility, attention or learning. A slot-center inspection verifies each slot's visible identity, not every frame inside every 18-frame interval. Source time continuity here is supported by visibly changing records and the ledger, not an independent full source-frame checksum audit.

Largest remaining gap and concrete repair condition
The largest perceptual gap is whether the sequence is actually learned before replacements, particularly the rapid 9.6–14.4 all-symbol cycle. My image method cannot certify that intended experience. The last substitution at 52.2 then gives FIELD only two image returns before the release; late records have much less opportunity to become anticipated actions than early ones.

Do not report those results until performed. Preserve equal slot durations and persistent mappings; do not add persistent numbering that substitutes a visible counter for the intended memory task. This is a proposed repair conditional on an observed learning failure, not a reason to declare the present construction failed from stills.

Material and lineage scope
The final FIELD record visibly includes placards and a collective street action. It is socially specific, not merely neutral moving texture; the source caption 'Working Together' preserves an identity but not much context in 0.6-second bursts. 19–33 A occupies the left panel while B/C spaces are empty. The parent is visibly a still copy of the reel-handling source. Its320x240 caption and100-parent-pixel bar identify digital dimensions, not the reel's size in millimetres.

Independent source and transformation checks
The original203.mp4 video stream probes as320x240. My independent source2.5-second PNG and carriers/A_parent.png are pixel-identical in all RGB channels. A is therefore correctly associated with the selected original frame, not simply assigned a source ID in prose.

Cropping A at(64,128,256,240) yields192x112 source pixels. My BOX reduction of that crop to48x28 is pixel-identical to carriers/C_crop_48x28.png. The selected aspect ratio is preserved:192/112 equals48/28. The display scales are internally coherent: A320x240 appears288x216 at0.9; C48x28 appears288x168 at6. The viewer can read the transformations relatively quickly; the image remains substantially the same during the long comparison. The ledger supplies four synthetic narration passages, but I have not heard their pace or their contribution, so I cannot certify that those holds are earned at native speed. The work risks functioning as an illustrated explanation more than a developing short.

Argument and limits
The actual progression is coherent: a reel handled in an archive becomes a decoded frame, an imposed selection, then a coarser derivative. The face exclusion and block formation make selection/resampling losses concrete. Removing the images while retaining the red mark and rulers separates editorial decisions from represented content. Actual actions and rules

At12 seconds outward-pointing brackets light up at left. Over the following seconds the sheet widens and the hands separate; by16 it is broad, with crease lines. That is a legible sign→opening relation. It is reinforced later around24–27 with another bracket cue and widening.

At20 seconds the circular-arrow sign lights above the tray. The folded sheet turns through oblique positions toward a quarter-turn, with hands following its edges. At34,40 and46 seconds the III sign on the tray is highlighted. The paper now retains many crease lines and irregular upper/lower edges, and small manipulations continue. The graphic III is not an obvious universal repeat symbol and can resemble a pause mark. The earlier responses do not give an emphatic full opening/closing cycle, so its exact 'repeat fold/unfold' meaning is less securely inferable than the bracket and turn rules. Refusal and final state

The face's pupils shift toward that action. The tray compresses toward a thin horizontal shape, then expands as a pale blank back around49–50. The sign disappears with its surface rather than simply vanishing at an unrelated location. By53–54 both hands again guard the folded sheet and the tray remains blank. That makes the refusal an alteration of the rule-bearing object, not merely a stopped animation.

The prior highlights occur at roughly six-second intervals, and the expected subsequent III highlight at52 seconds is absent. That omission is consistent with the tray losing its sign face. The table stays behind these actions and the sign occupies the tray surface that is flipped. I found no blocking layer inversion in the inspected samples.
